Request horses for presentation to the new Viceroy of Goa and the new Governor of Surat, ‘which some say is in great esteeme with the King;’ also a supply for the King of Golconda, which the Masulipatam factors are always urging. Refer to the abuse discovered in merchants shipping aboard the <em>Blessing</em> last year ‘zarbaffes [<em>zar-bāf</em>, cloth of gold], camerbands [<em>kamarband</em>, a girdle], velvetts, taffataes, etc., in place or under the appearance of rosewater’. As the Company is thus defrauded both in freight and customs, it is suggested that either a few packages should be opened as a test, or else the merchant should be required to sign a declaration that the merchandise is only rose-water, and then, should it ‘bee discovered to bee gomaash’ [Pers. <em>qumāsh</em>, fine goods], the proper freight should be exacted. Suspect some fraud in the freight-goods now laden on the <em>William</em>, but have not opened any bales, as that would drive the merchants to ship by the Dutch instead. However, should there be any fighting between the Dutch and the Portuguese, the natives would ‘so fawne upon us to transport them that wee might well introduce a reformacion’. Advise consultation with the Shāhbandar at Gombroon to check the fraud on the customs. Entreat them to dispose to advantage of the goods belonging to the Captain of Damān, and return the proceeds in horses. Request them to buy the Japanese copper, if it may be had at 30 or 31 lārīs the Surat maund, as it will fetch at Goa 23¾ rials the quintal (which is four maunds). This would be better than sending money to Surat. Intended dispatch of the <em>Francis</em> (80 tons) to Basrā. Note the silk and rhubarb laden on the <em>Blessing</em>.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-212"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="212" data-folio="166" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n211_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 166">[p. 166]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Regret to learn the ill success of the goods received from Masulipatam. Censure their remissness in not sending their accounts (which are now three years behind), and ‘peremptorily require’ the dispatch of those for 1633 and 1634 by the <em>William</em> without fail. That vessel has commission to take a freight for Surat, Dābhol, or Masulipatam, whichever may be most profitable. Of the Dutch fleet from Gombroon, the admiral, the <em>Amboina</em>, is destined for Holland, and went over the bar on January 30 ; the other four are waiting here for freight. Movements of the English ships. Request a supply of rosewater, Shīrāz wine, and pistachios. Have sent the Agent for his table three bags of the finest rice, ‘heere called commoot <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-024" href="#sec-06-fn-024" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-024" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>,’ and will gladly forward anything else of the kind which the factors may desire. Find now that they cannot spare any rials ; and, as they are likely to be very short of money, they must require the dispatch by the <em>William</em> of whatever the Masulipatam consignment has produced, and also the return of the two chests of rials forwarded in the <em>Blessing</em>. Will send back the proceeds later in the year without fail. In buying rice, tobacco, etc., they are always required to pay extra for the bags or wrappings, or else provide new ones ; suggest therefore that when these goods are sold an equivalent charge may be made. Certain bales to be disposed of on behalf of the Vedor da Fazenda at Goa, and the proceeds to be returned in horses, carpets, or money. Dispute as to a bale of sugar. Claim by Mr. White on account of a present to the Sultan of Gombroon. Would be glad of some ‘acharr [pickle : Pers. <em>āchār</em>] of onyons, garlick, and capers’. <em>PS.</em> (<em>February</em> 4)—The <em>Francis</em> has arrived from Dābhol. Enclose a list of prices current there. Lead to be returned by the <em>William</em>. The passengers to be numbered and care taken that they do not land goods as personal baggage. <em>PPS.</em>—Their tin, if not vendible in Persia, can be disposed of in India or at Basrā. (<em>Copy.</em> 14¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">INSTRUCTIONS FROM PRESIDENT METHWOLD AND COUNCIL TO JOHN DRAKE, FEBRUARY 3, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 424).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To proceed to Goa in the <em>Blessing</em> to fetch the promised pepper. On arrival he should deliver the accompanying letters to the Viceroy <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-213" data-page="213" data-folio="167" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n212_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 167">[p. 167]</button> and the Vedor, requesting its early delivery; and while waiting he may dispose of the oil, cotton wool, and tobacco. The last-named being 'monopolized' at Goa, he may only sell to the 'contractor'; if he will not take it, Drake may either get rid of it at Dābhol or bring it back. It was arranged last year that payment for the pepper should be made either in pagodas or in 'St. Tomaes' (at 102 for 100 pagodas); this is more profitable than paying in rials of eight at 9½ 'tangoes'. The Vedor has been asked to deliver to Drake the rials due for the copper previously delivered; and in addition he will receive herewith 2,000 'Venetians' and 3,000 English 20<em>s.</em> pieces. The former, which can be bought at Surat for 8½ mahmūdīs 2 pice, 'were lately woorth in Goa 18½ tangoes the peece, which is but half a tangoe (which wee vallew to bee neerest 3<em>d.</em> sterling) lesse then two rialls of eight.' Drake should pay for the pepper in gold; and then demand of the Vedor the 11,844 rials due for the copper, 4,088 'seraphims' for the wheat, and 3,000 'pardoaes' left there by the late Viceroy to pay for a coach and horses to be provided in London. The latter amount should be received in rials of eight. The Vedor had also a quantity of lead from the <em>London</em>; the proceeds and any remainder should be demanded. The <em>London</em> left two anchors which the Vedor wished to buy, and a supply of pitch and tar is now sent at his request; an account should be taken, allowing him at his own rates for certain stores supplied to English ships. Drake is not to stay longer at Goa than February 14, unless they decide (on hearing from him) to let the <em>Discovery</em> call there on her way to England. If no pepper is to be had, the <em>Blessing</em> must return at once. Should she meet the <em>London</em> on her way, her silk, etc., may be transferred to that vessel. At Goa use may be made of a Banyan named 'Harry Metta' [Hari Mehtā], to whom his master, 'our broker Chout,' has sent instructions to assist them. A number of dutty wheat sacks were left in the charge of the 'Guardian Major'; these may be used for the pepper. The invoice gives full particulars of the cargo. (<em>Copy. 5 pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Commission and Instructions to John White, Commander of the</span> <em>Blessing</em>, <span class="sc">February</span> 3, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 429).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To do his best to return by the end of the month, in order that his goods may be transferred to the <em>Discovery</em> for dispatch to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-214" data-page="214" data-folio="168" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n213_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 168">[p. 168]</button> England. He should, if possible, call at Dāman to receive on board a servant of the Captain of that town. The management of affairs at Goa is committed to Drake. Should the <em>London</em> be met, with the pepper on board, he is to return with her. If possible, inquiry should be made at Dābhol for the <em>Francis</em>, of which no news has been heard. To take care of the behaviour of his crew at Goa. Not to remain there after February 14. If he is delayed, the <em>Discovery</em> will be sent to meet him. (<em>Copy. 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Commission and Instructions to Christopher Brown</span>, <span class="sc">Commander of the</span> <em>WILLIAM</em>, <span class="sc">February</span> 3, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 432).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To sail at once for Gombroon, and there to embark any freight-goods that may be ready, including goods for Dābhol or Masulipatam, to which place he is to proceed on his return. As the King of Golconda has granted the English exemption from customs, it will be advantageous to give a preference to goods for Masulipatam. Returning, he is, if possible, to anchor at the Bar of Surat, and send up his letters; but should the weather be unfavourable, he may proceed to Dābhol instead and dispatch letters to Surat overland. If absolutely necessary, he may go straight to Masulipatam. From the latter place he will be sent to Gombroon and Surat again. The Company's sea commission is to be read to the crew the first Monday in every month. Private trade to be prevented. (<em>Copy. 4 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Pass issued by President Methwold and Council at Surat, February</span> 8, 1636<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-025" href="#sec-06-fn-025" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-025" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 174).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To Nākhudā Daulat Mahmūd, of the <em>Salāmatī</em>, belonging to Hakīm Masīh-uz-Zamān, and bound for Basrā. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, February</span> 12, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 71).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote two days ago<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-026" href="#sec-06-fn-026" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-026" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> instructing them to remit 50,000 rupees to Agra before the Dutch could arrive there. The latter intend to dispatch a caravan of 15 Hollanders in four days hence, and it was <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-215" data-page="215" data-folio="169" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n214_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 169">[p. 169]</button> at first in contemplation to send three Englishmen with them to carry up 80,000 rupees; now, however, they have accepted an offer from Tapī Dās to give them bills on Agra for 50,000 rupees ‘at one half per cent. losse’, and accordingly they cancel their former instructions. Will still send with the Dutch caravan 20,000 rupees for Ahmadābād and 10,000 for Baroda. If the remittance to Agra has already been made, they must write to ‘Dangee’ to send the money back again by exchange. <em>PS.</em>—‘Cullian Parrack [Kalyān Pārak] is come hither to Surratt and doth much lament his condition that, having beene now 25 yeares the Companies sheroff in Amadavad, and from whence hee reaped some creditt though little profitt, hee is now in a manner expulsed and so much disgrasd. It is the Companies positive order that there cash should bee kept in theire owne house and by there owne servants, which wee will not dispense withall’; but as he already ‘receiveth the mony, peruseth it, and delivers it into your custody, soe, when you have occation to pay it out, hee desires to doe it, in your sight and in your owne house’. He wishes this ‘for his reputations sake’; and, as they are willing to gratify an ancient servant, they would be glad to learn whether the factors see any objection. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">INSTRUCTIONS FROM PRESIDENT METHWOLD AND COUNCIL TO WILLIAM COLLARD, FEBRUARY 18, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 72).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is placed in command of the other Englishmen accompanying the caravan to Ahmadābād. Of the eight chests, containing 80,000 new rupees, he is to leave one at Baroda and take on the rest to his destination, keeping company with the Dutch. No trouble is expected at the Broach customhouse, ‘since mony paies nothing there,’ and his only goods are a few bales for the Padres at Agra. He must keep close to the treasure, though it is not likely that any assault will be made, ‘especially if you doe but seeme resolute and watchfull.’ The movements of the caravan will be settled by the Dutch merchant, Arent Barentszoon, ‘who is a discreet and temperate man and hath many times past that way with caphilaes of great charge.’ For expenses on the journey 200 rupees are delivered him. He is to be back by March 10. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-216"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="216" data-folio="170" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n215_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 170">[p. 170]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to the Merchants of the</span> <em>London</em> [<span class="sc">at Goa</span>], <span class="sc">February</span> 19, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 74).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Welcome their return, and look forward to their speedy arrival at this port. If they have not started before the receipt of the present letter, they must at once set sail. Any money due for freight should be brought hither in rials. Will supply them with provisions and stores on arrival. (<em>Copy. 2½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Baroda, February</span> 19, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 73).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Trust that they have reached Baroda safely and have made good progress with their business, for which purpose they now send them 10,000 rupees. Have paid their bill of exchange. Calicoes to be sent from Broach. Some blue baftas bought at Surat; so fewer will be wanted from Baroda. Transmit a letter from Goa announcing the return of the <em>London</em>. News has arrived from Masulipatam of the death of Agent Joyce on December 29. The caravan, attended by fourteen Hollanders and three Englishmen, passed ‘the river of Beriaw’ [Variao] last night. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Ahmadābād, February</span> 20, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 77).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send them 20,000 rupees for investment, and 50,000 to discharge their engagements for the sum remitted to Agra. Hope to join them after the dispatch of the ships. Desire the speedy return of Collard and his companions. The <em>London</em> has reached Goa. As she may possibly be sent home immediately, a quantity of saltpetre should be bought and sent to Cambay, for shipment to Swally. Agent Joyce is dead at Masulipatam. Certain packs sent by the caravan for transmission to the Padres at Agra, containing books, church ornaments, wine, strong waters, physic, and ‘one tynne pott sowed upp in gunny, which conteyneth a composition which they call chuculatte<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-027" href="#sec-06-fn-027" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-027" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>.’ (<em>Copy. 2¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-217"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="217" data-folio="171" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n216_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 171">[p. 171]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Forder's Account of the Voyage of the <em>Discovery</em> from Gombroon to Surat</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. lx. p. 185).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-028" href="#sec-06-fn-028" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-028" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1636, <em>February</em> 22. Took in silk and passengers for Surat. <em>February</em> 23. Three Dutch ships arrived. <em>February</em> 25. Set sail, having 73 passengers on board. Two Surat frigates set sail with them. <em>March</em> 14. Saw the coast of India near Sanjān. <em>March</em> 16. Anchored in Swally Road. In the Hole were the <em>Blessing</em>, five Dutch ships, and two Surat junks. <em>March</em> 17. The <em>Discovery</em> went into the Hole, and the President came on board. <em>March</em> 30. The <em>London</em> anchored in the Road. <em>March</em> 31. She came into the Hole. (5 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to the Factors at Baroda, February</span> 26, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 80).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan has arrived in company of Wilson. Regret Collard's detention by sickness. Answer various points in their letters. The Governor of Surat has farmed the customs of Broach, which means the appointment of a new Customer. Calico to be viewed at Broach. Six Dutch vessels arrived at Swally yesterday. The <em>Blessing</em> has returned from Goa without any pepper. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to John White, Commander of the</span> <em><span class="sc">Blessing</span> </em>[<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">February</span> 27, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 81).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Congratulate his safe arrival. Expect the <em>London</em> shortly, and will then come down. Supply of sailcloth, called 'narrowe sareiaes' [<em>see</em> p. 130]. Regret that White has not been able to procure any pepper, but this cannot be helped. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Ahmadābād, February</span> 27, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 82).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan has arrived. The brokers should be punished for their frauds; 'only take care that you fall not too heavy uppon theire persons in corporall punishment, wee meane in chawbucking<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-029" href="#sec-06-fn-029" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-029" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> them, least, being guilty and in that respect desperate persons, they may consent to loose themselves that you may find trouble; in which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-218" data-page="218" data-folio="172" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n217_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 172">[p. 172]</button> particuler wee doe rather approove that you should deliver them over unto the Mahumetan power; but therin also wee would not have you to bee too forward, since wee know that nothing will bee taken from them in that way which will bee returned for our sattisfaction.' Those who gave information should in the meantime be employed (if capable). Have long suspected a combination 'amongst our tribe of brokers', and are resolved to 'dissolve the knott'. Although 'Panjew' has been the Company's servant for over twenty years, if anything can be proved against him he shall never be employed again. It seems that the 'addowayes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-030" href="#sec-06-fn-030" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-030" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>' are in league with the brokers, and there is reason to fear that these frauds have been long practised in divers places, and have been connived at by factors, whose private trade has been in consequence transported either gratis or for very little. To bring goods from Sind or by way of Cambay would remedy much of this, and if peace be continued with the Portuguese they will try those ways next year. Approve the excess of indigo and blue baftas. No further news from 'Dangee' at Agra; but the Hollanders report that 'Signor Tymeers' [François Timmers] sent off an indigo caravan on January 10; probably the English goods come therewith and are now not far from Ahmadābād. Thank them for information regarding the value of gold. On the last rupees the 'vatawe' [<em>see</em> p. 68] was four mahmūdīs the hundred rupees. Await a full report concerning the brokers, together with Panjew's defence, 'for wee will heare with both eares.' Will be watchful as regards 'Chout', and 'if wee can tripp him, hee shall fall with his cozen german'. Nothing is said here about Saif Khān; but it is supposed that the King will winter at Ahmadābād, 'and so hee may bee ecclipsed by the greater light.' 'It is said that some officers of the Kings past not long since neere unto this towne to measure the distance and most comodious places of the Kings gifts [<em>sic</em>], from whence the opinion of his returning through Guzeratt is much confirmed.' May find it necessary to send some person of quality to him to remonstrate about a farmān lately procured by the Governor of Surat, imposing a number of restraints upon them. Will never consent to such conditions of captivity, but must temporize until their goods are embarked for England; after which they will 'sue for redresse above or enforce it hereabouts', in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-219" data-page="219" data-folio="173" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n218_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 173">[p. 173]</button> association with the Dutch, who are equally indignant. At the same time was read another parwāna, declaring the indigo trade free to all traders. Arrival yesterday of a Dutch fleet from Batavia. Great mortality among them. The <em>Michael</em> has returned from Diu, ‘where wee have turned the penny and gotten only the sight and acquaintaince of another Portugall Captayne and his towne and castle.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 5¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Company to the President and Council at Bantam</span>, <span class="sc">February</span> 29, 1636 (<em>Letter Books</em>, vol. i. p. 82).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote on November 30<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-031" href="#sec-06-fn-031" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-031" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> by the <em>Swan</em>, which, however, did not sail until February 5. Now send them the <em>Hart</em> of 500 tons, under John Scallon. . . . Cloves and sugar to be sent to the Coast for sale there or transport to Persia. . . . Are writing by the <em>Mary</em> to Surat, directing the dispatch of some factors from thence to Bantam. . . . A youth named Henry Greenhill<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-032" href="#sec-06-fn-032" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-032" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> went out in the <em>Jonas</em> on her last voyage as an attendant upon Captain Swanley, and was left at Masulipatam. His friends desire his return, which is accordingly ordered. . . . Edward Collett, who is at the Coast, is to be given preferment if found worthy. Four ships (the <em>Dragon</em>, <em>Sun</em>, <em>Katherine</em>, and <em>Planter</em>) are about to start, as is suspected, for the East, as Captain Weddell and Swanley and some lately returned factors are going in them. It is reported that their voyage is for Goa, Malacca, and China. Warn them to furnish no supplies to any such English (or Dutch) vessels, but to reserve them for the Company’s ships. (<em>Extracts only.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Consultation held in Surat by President Methwold and Messrs. Breton and Peirson</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 1, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 436).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The frigate <em>Francis</em> is to be employed in a voyage to Masulipatam when the season permits. Meanwhile she is to proceed to Dābhol, where Mīr Kamāldīn and other merchants will freight her with pepper for Gombroon. She will then take in any freight-goods that may be ready there and proceed either to Dābhol or to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-220" data-page="220" data-folio="174" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n219_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 174">[p. 174]</button> Masulipatam. John Oldfield is appointed her purser and steward. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, March</span> 1, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 87).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send back their house servant who came with the caravan, as he is 'to us only chargeable by his roza' [Hind. <em>rozī</em>, daily allowance]. Have given him five rupees 'enaeme' [Arabic <em>inām</em>, a gift]. Heard to-day of the death of William Collard. Trust that the money has been remitted to Agra as directed, and that the indigo so dearly purchased is now near or at Ahmadābād; some of it they may send to Basrā and the rest to England. News from Persia. Inquire whether any saltpetre has been bought. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Baroda, March</span> 1, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 89).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lament the death of Collard. Disposal of his effects. Provision of piece-goods for dispatch to England. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to Andrew Warden, Master of the</span> <em>FRANCIS</em> [<span class="sc">March</span> 1 ?, 1636] (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 90).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is to prepare for a voyage of some months. Provisions sent down, and boards to make bulkheads. The bearer, John Oldfield, has been appointed purser and steward. Goods to be transferred to the <em>Blessing</em>. Mr. White will provide him with a mate. (<em>The rest is missing. Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Lee, Thomas Fletcher, John Hitchins, Richard Fitch, and Robert Carpenter, aboard the</span> <em>CRISPIANA</em> [<span class="sc">at Johanna</span>], <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">the Commanders of the next ships</span>], <span class="sc">March</span> 2, 1636 (<em>O.C.</em> 1574).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Left St. Augustine's Bay on January 22, but owing to bad weather they did not reach this road till February 20. Hearing of the wreck of an English ship at Mohilla, they sent a boat thither, which brought back news that the vessel was the <em>Samaritan</em> of London, commanded by William Cobb. The latter's colleague, William Ayres, came into this road the last day of February in the <em>Roebuck</em>. On examining his commission, they found that he had been sent out by <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-221" data-page="221" data-folio="175" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n220_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 175">[p. 175]</button> the King's authority, and so they desisted from further interference; but they have heard from the natives that the <em>Roebuck</em> has taken three junks in the Red Sea and intends (after visiting Madagascar) to go thither once more; also that Cobb is building a small vessel at Mohilla for the same design. Are now about to set sail for Surat; should they fail to gain that port, they will put into some harbour in Portuguese India. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Commission and Instructions from the President and Council at Surat to Andrew Warden, Master of the <em>Francis</em>, March</span> 4, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 437).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To proceed to Dābhol, and there embark 'a fraight of Moores goods' for Gombroon, and 'an auntient Persian named Mier Camaladyne, whome some of us have knowne almost 20 yeares to have bin a powerfull and most constant freind to our whole nation in Mesulapatam'. Every attention is to be shown to him. At Gombroon Warden will receive directions for his further proceedings. Should he be sent to Dābhol, he is to leave letters for Surat there with 'Vemuldas' [Vimal Dās]; if to Masulipatam, the Agent there will take charge of them. At the latter place Warden will find instructions from Surat for his next employment, which will probably be to Bengal. Pitt goes with him to Dābhol to supervise the lading of the vessel. Private trade is to be prevented by every means in his power. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Instructions to William Pitt, proceeding to Dābhol, March</span> 5, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 439).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is to go again to Dābhol, partly to finish the sale of the goods previously sent, but chiefly to see to the lading of the <em>Francis</em> for Gombroon. On arrival he is to resume possession of the goods he left with 'Vemuldas', sell them on the best terms he can, and either invest the proceeds in pepper or await instructions. The same course is to be followed with the money received for freight. As regards the latter, he is to demand 50 'larrees' per candy of pepper, 'or more or lesse as the custome of the country will affoard.' For passengers the usual rates should be charged, except in the case of Mīr Kamāldīn, who has been offered free accommodation. No private trade to be stowed in the hold. He is to send information <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-222" data-page="222" data-folio="176" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n221_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 176">[p. 176]</button> what goods are vendible either at Dābhol or ‘above in the country’, for if his report be favourable it is probable that he will be sent inland, ‘where you may remaine untill the winter shalbe past over, and make a full tryall what the country will affoard.’ Should Mīr Kamāldīn and the other merchants have departed before the <em>Francis</em> arrives, Pitt is merely to sell the goods and bring back the proceeds, either in pepper or in ‘larrees’. ‘The frequent resort of Mallabarr merchants unto that port hath put into our thoughts an imaginacion that in proba[bi]llitie they may bringe thither quantety of such Guiney shells as herewithall wee give unto you a muster. They are in this country called cawries [<em>see the last volume</em>, pp. 275, 287], and above at Agra passe for small mony, as badams [bitter almonds: Hind. <em>badām</em>] in these parts. They are required for England in good quantetyes, and in Bengala they are most currant. Wee bought about twelve months since a parcell at ma[hmūdīs] 4⅞ the maen of this place.’ He may take all he can get at about that price. The ships bound for Bantam will call at Dābhol; he should therefore keep Surat informed of the prices, etc., of commodities, and of the progress of his business. He is to endeavour to procure a farmān from the King with a grant of privileges. Another factor will possibly be sent to assist him. Recommend ‘frugallity in all things’ and attention to his religious duties. (<em>Copy.</em> 3½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-033" href="#sec-06-fn-033" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-033" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">at Surat to the Company, March</span> 6, 1636 (<em>O.C.</em> 1553).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote on November 25 by the <em>Blessing</em>, on January 2 by the <em>Hendrik Frederik</em>, on January 29 by the <em>Amboina</em>, and on February 3 [<em>sic</em>] by the Portuguese carracks; now send a further letter by way of Persia. Owing to the non-arrival of the <em>Discovery</em>, <em>Hopewell</em>, and <em>London</em>, they fear that they will not be able to dispatch a ship to England before March 20. This may make it impossible for her to get round the Cape in time, and in that case she must winter at either Mauritius or St. Augustine’s Bay. Briefly recapitulate the course of events since the <em>Jonas</em> and her consorts left Goa on January 21, 1635. The President returned to Surat in the <em>Hopewell</em> on February 21; and on March 8 that ship was dispatched to Gombroon with freight-goods producing ‘neere 7,000 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-223" data-page="223" data-folio="177" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n222_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 177">[p. 177]</button> mamothes', besides a quantity of sugar on the Company's account, which cost 13¾ mahmūdīs and realized 28 'larrees' per maund at Gombroon. The <em>Reformation</em> started on March 25 for Sumatra with a cargo invoiced at 118,520 mahmūdīs. On the following day the <em>London</em> got back from Persia, and on the day after that the <em>Blessing</em> arrived. On April 9 the <em>London</em> set sail for Goa and 'Macau in China'; while four days later the <em>Blessing</em> departed for Bantam. The <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Speedwell</em>, having made a tedious passage from Masulipatam, arrived at Gombroon so late that they resolved to winter at 'Bombayee'. The <em>Discovery</em> anchored there on May 2; but the <em>Speedwell</em> failed to get in and was forced to proceed to Goa. There she obtained supplies and, sailing again on the 8th, arrived in safety at Masulipatam, whence she was at once sent on to 'Bengala'. 'Conceive the winter monzoone now blowne over, and this disconsolate province of Guzeratt exceedeingly refreshed with aboundant raines; which have falne soe seasonably as to produce aboundance of all sorts of graine, which hath occationed a cheapnesse of them under the price of those times which preceeded the late great calamety.' Built two pinnaces at Damān of 80 or 100 tons burden, named the <em>Michael</em> and the <em>Francis</em>; also two smaller boats at Bassein. The <em>Discovery</em> reached Swally on October 21, and on November 2 was sent with the <em>Bassein</em> to 'Sinda'. On November 11 the <em>Blessing</em> returned from Bantam; whereupon she was at once laden for Gombroon. Before she left, the <em>William</em> came in from England, followed by the <em>Hopewell</em> on November 21. Both vessels sailed again on December 9, the former for Goa and the latter for 'Sinda'. The <em>William</em> returned on January 10 [1636] without the pepper she went to fetch. She brought news that none was to be hoped for from Cochin and Quilon, owing to the great drought; also a complimentary letter from the new Viceroy, Pero da Silva, who had reached Goa with two carracks and one small ship. On January 19 the <em>Bassein</em> arrived from 'Sinda', bringing intelligence that the <em>Discovery</em> had left for Persia on January 6, and that the <em>Hopewell</em>, which had reached that port in safety, proposed to make a voyage to 'Cacha' [Cutch] before starting for Surat. On January 28 the <em>Blessing</em> returned from Gombroon with a lading of silk and rhubarb. Five days later the <em>William</em> sailed for that port with a freight of nearly 20,000 mahmūdīs in Moors' goods; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-224" data-page="224" data-folio="178" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n223_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 178">[p. 178]</button> and at the same time the <em>Blessing</em> started for Goa to fetch the promised pepper. The two pinnaces, <em>Michael</em> and <em>Francis</em>, came into Swally Road on December 19, and were then sent, the former to Diu and the latter to Dābhol; ‘which, being more intended for discovery then proffit, had their ends, for they are returned with more satisfaction then advance.’ On February 26 the <em>Blessing</em> came back from Goa without any pepper. The <em>London</em> was found at Goa, having arrived there January 27 on her way back from China with a miscellaneous cargo, including goods for the Company invoiced at 30,479 rials of eight. The <em>Francis</em> brought back from Dābhol ‘a loveing invitation to resort unto, or to reside in, that port or any place of the country; but wee have noe great encouragement, for, notwithstanding the small proportion of goodes which wee sent in the <em>Francis</em>, and allmost two months time which our merchant attended their sale, hee is returned leaveing the greater parte undisposed of; as allso without the Kinges firmaen, which wee purposely sent to seeke. But in place thereof (besides our expectation) hee bringes a letter from an auntient and assured freind unto our nation, Meere Camaladyne, whom you have soe often heard named from Mesulapatam; which letter, togather with the profer of considerable fraight, doth exceedeingly importune some accomodation for to transport him from thence unto Gombroone, whether hee is permitted by the Kinge of Golcundah to passe (after great sollicitation of himselfe and freinds) that hee may visitt the toombe of a certaine prophett unto which hee is much devoted. Wee considered the motion, and presently apprehended the probable benefitt of 10,000 larees fraight to bee gotten (togather with this opertunitie of advise) without any other inconvenience or losse of other employment, since wee had almost resolved before to have sent her to Mesulapatam, and from thence to Bengall, from whence wee expect her back againe here in January following with lacke, sugar, and such other goodes as in probabillitie will yeald in Balsora [Basrā] exceedeing great profitt. And this course wee did rather make choyce of then to lay her up to winter here unprofitably the whole season of the raines; wherein wee are now confirmed, and doe not doubt but from Dabull to Gombroone and from Gombroone back unto Dabull or Mesulapatam, shee may very well advance in fraight at least 800<em>l.</em> sterling, and yet be at Mesulapatam in May or June, in a <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-225" data-page="225" data-folio="179" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n224_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 179">[p. 179]</button> very good season (yea the best season) to goe for Bengala; from whence shee may returne againe in October by the way of Mesulapatam, and arrive here at Surratt in December or January.’ The Persian accounts are now three years in arrear. Express doubts as to the wisdom of continuing the contract with the Shāh. ‘The trade of India hath of late daies bin desperate enough’; but it is improving and they trust it will return by degrees to its pristine condition. Cannot tell at present what they will be able to send home; but will consider the matter on the arrival of the <em>London</em>, <em>Discovery</em>, and <em>Hopewell</em>. One of the three ships<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-034" href="#sec-06-fn-034" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-034" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> will go home; the other two will be sent to Bantam, one going direct and the other by way of Masulipatam. The President and Council at Bantam are confident of being able to lade both of them; and have also demanded goods and money from Surat to the value of 80,000 rials of eight, ‘in which poynt wee must interpose your more prevalent comands.’ The <em>Hopewell</em> is destined for Sumatra, unless some more profitable employment presents itself. The <em>William</em> will go to Masulipatam and thence with freight-goods to Gombroon, returning to Surat in November to lade for England. Before the following February they hope to be in a position to send home also the <em>Crispiana</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-035" href="#sec-06-fn-035" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-035" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (should she arrive) or some other ship. Stock only is wanting; but they count on the Company supplying them, and in the meantime will borrow with discretion. ‘Wee have in some of our former letters touched upon an intent which wee had to dispeede away one of our pinnaces unto Balsora [Basrā], which lyes in the bottome of the Persian Gulphe; and now being confirmed in the assured hopes of desired profitt by the concurrence of all intelligence which wee have sought from severall persons and places, wee have gott togather from Amadavad and elsewhere an apt cargazoone, according as wee have bin instructed; which by Gods assistance wee doe intend to lade upon the <em>Michaell</em> and to dispeede her from hence within tenn dayes after date hereof.’ By her they will send a copy of the present letter (with additions), as, ‘for the certainety and expedition of the conveighance, wee heare that way is much better then this of Persia.’ The Portuguese continually use that route, and it is stated that letters from Goa so sent have reached Madrid within <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-226" data-page="226" data-folio="180" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n225_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 180">[p. 180]</button> four months. The <em>Bassein</em>, which is about 25 tons, will accompany the <em>Michael</em> thither; and then the only vessel left at Surat will be the smaller boat (of about eight tons) of the two built at Bassein, which they have named the <em>Kit</em>. She is ordinarily employed in the river between Surat and Swally; and when well manned, is ‘a shipp of warr, which the Mallabarrs dare but looke upon, and that at distance.’ ‘For your factoryes, they are lame in all places for want of better or almost any assistance.’ Bornford, Wylde, and Aldington will be back shortly; ‘yet you will want experienced men, both here and in most of your subordinate factoryes. Some time wilbe required in them which are most capable. In the meane time some doe still drop away; which should bee supplyed from a stock of ingenious young men, bredd up one under another. Thomas Thimbleby, Ambrose Taylor, and Richard Swanly wee have elsewhere advised to have paid their debts to nature in these parts; whom William Collard hath lately followed, and in him wee have lost a helpefull and hopefull young man. In Mesulapatam your Agent, Thomas Joyce, deceased the 29th of December last; whose losse wee lamment with affectionate sorrow, for surely hee was a man of very great abillities, exceedeingly loved and esteemed in that place where hee last served you, both in his life and death; whom now Gerald Pinson doth succeede. About the same time William Favor died at Golcondah; and how many of the <em>Speedewells</em> company in Bengala, our former by one of the Dutch ships hath informed you.’ They understand that John Hunter has died at Macassar, and that he has been succeeded by Malachi Martin. Their informant, a Portuguese, ‘jested out this description . . . There is dead (saith hee) at Macasser the English Captaine. I cannott name him, butt it is hee that wore his head on one side and his hatt on the other: hee that could play on the lute and dance; whom a very little man doth succeede.’ Some of the older factors here are anxious to go home; among whom they have prevailed upon John Drake to stay, by giving him a gratification of 40<em>l.</em> per annum, which they trust will be approved. Fremlen prepares for England; but they cannot do without him, on account of his experience and his linguistic abilities, and so ‘will either win his stay or enforce it’. Nathaniel Wyche and Ralph Cartwright both expect to go home from Masulipatam; while John Yard, who succeeds Cartwright in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-227" data-page="227" data-folio="181" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n226_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 181">[p. 181]</button> Bengal, pretends that his time is expired and desires leave to return. This intelligence is sent in order that the Company may take the necessary measures to supply the vacancies in good time. Methwold avails himself of the opportunity to entreat the Company to provide a successor to his own post. ‘A second vow made to a greived wife procured him consent to contract for a time of absence, with condition, sollemnely protested before wittnesse, not to exceede that time. Hee accompts his vow sanctemonious, and dare not consent to infringe it ; although hee professeth that in all other respects of health, esteeme, and reward hee is soe happely (though undeservedly) gratefied that hee should rather make it his suite to renew his covenant, and willingly end his daies in the performance of his endlesse obligations.’ He would have written a private letter to this effect, but ‘such are not proper for land conveighances’. He has yet, however, almost two years to serve, at the end of which hee will make bold to embark for England. (8 <em>pp. Received September</em> 16, 1637.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Fremlen aboard the</span> <em>HOPEWELL</em> [<span class="sc">near Lārībandar</span>] <span class="sc">to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 10, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 590).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Left Tatta on February 23, reached ‘Bandur’ [Lārībandar] two days later, and sent the goods on board next day. They have tried in vain to get over the bar, owing to violent winds, but trust to sail shortly. The goods laden aboard the ship are calicoes, cotton yarn, and indigo for England, and ‘googur<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-036" href="#sec-06-fn-036" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-036" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, ‘hing [Hind. <em>hīng</em>] or arsefœtida’, ‘purwasse’, and ‘combera’, which are gross commodities vendible in Gujarāt. Spiller has been dangerously ill but is now better. The goods, etc., at Tatta have been left in the charge of ‘Lickmidas’. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp. Received overland March</em> 27.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to John Drake at Swally Marine, March</span> 13, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 91).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Lading of the <em>Michael</em>. When this is arranged, he may return to Surat to prepare for his voyage. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-228"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="228" data-folio="182" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n227_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 182">[p. 182]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3">P<span class="sc">resident</span> M<span class="sc">ethwold and</span> C<span class="sc">ouncil at</span> S<span class="sc">urat to</span> J<span class="sc">ohn</span> W<span class="sc">hite</span> [AT SWALLY], M<span class="sc">arch</span> 13, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 91).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Repair, lading, and provisioning of the <em>Michael</em>. Dispatch of the <em>Kit</em> to Cambay to fetch indigo and saltpetre. The <em>Bassein</em> is to come to Surat for some casks and rice. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">T<span class="sc">he</span> S<span class="sc">ame to the</span> F<span class="sc">actors at</span> A<span class="sc">hmadābād</span>, M<span class="sc">arch</span> 15, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 93).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on the 13th. Dispatch of the indigo and saltpetre. Trust that they have not refused the latter on account of the Nawāb’s stipulation for a gratuity of 200 rupees. ‘Saltpeeter is a prohibited commodity, and hath allwaies in all places paid some acknowledgment to the Governer of the place where it was bought.’ Are satisfied of the guilt of the brokers; but, if it be true that Robinson has beaten ‘Panjewe’ or kept him imprisoned for three days without rice or water till he gave his ‘screet’ for 2,000 rupees, he has done wrong and it is not unreasonable that the Governor should question it. Cannot believe that he has practised such rigour when they had expressly forbidden it. As matters stand, they direct that ‘Panjewe’ be dismissed from the Company’s service at Ahmadābād; and, if he can be proved guilty of fraud, he shall never be employed elsewhere. No bribes should be given; they have already been bountiful to Saif Khān, and will probably have to make him a present if they come to Ahmadābād. Note the remittance of 50,000 rupees to Agra. The <em>Discovery</em> is on her way back from Persia. (<em>Copy. 3½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">T<span class="sc">he</span> S<span class="sc">ame to the</span> F<span class="sc">actors at</span> B<span class="sc">aroda</span>, M<span class="sc">arch</span> 15, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 97).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Approve of their purchases of piece-goods. The Broach cloth is very poor. Request information as to the commission taken by the brokers at each place. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">T<span class="sc">he</span> S<span class="sc">ame to</span> J<span class="sc">ohn</span> W<span class="sc">hite</span> [AT SWALLY], M<span class="sc">arch</span> 16, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 98).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Enclose a letter to be given to the commander of the <em>Discovery</em> on arrival. Provisioning of the <em>Michael</em>. Supply of boards. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-229"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="229" data-folio="183" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n228_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 183">[p. 183]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to Richard Lucas, Master of the <em>Discovery</em></span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 16, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 99).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is not to land any passengers or their goods until further order. (<em>Copy.</em>  ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Benjamin Robinson and Edward Abbot at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 17, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 587).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The twenty bales of Agra indigo were dispatched by the Dutch caravan yesterday. Cannot get licence to transport the saltpetre. The trouble with the brokers. Desire that one be sent them from Surat. Medicine wanted for a native with sore eyes. ‘The Governor this day hath sent out a lasker [army: Pers. <em>lashkar</em>] of 1,000 horses against the Cuttees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-037" href="#sec-06-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> towards Dulkah [Dholka] where Mirza Isay’s<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-038" href="#sec-06-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> lasker of Soratt meets him.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 2¼ <em>pp. Received March</em> 23.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Abel Druce and Joseph Downham at Broach to the Same, March</span> 21, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 587).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(<em>First part missing.</em>) ‘Tulcedas’ [Tulsī Dās] will give no information against ‘Dew Doce’. (<em>Copy.</em>  ¼ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, March</span> 22, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 589).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Trouble in getting their goods passed through the customhouse. (<em>Copy.</em>  ½ <em>p. Received March</em> 24.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Benjamin Robinson and Edward Abbot at Ahmadābād to the Same, March</span> 22, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 592).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan from Agra was sent on to Surat yesterday. The saltpetre was dispatched at the same time, as they could not get licence for its transportation via Cambay, and moreover that route is at least as expensive as the other. Deny that ‘Panjew’ was so ill-treated as is alleged. The Governor has forced the Dutch to pay <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-230" data-page="230" data-folio="184" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n229_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 184">[p. 184]</button> 500 rupees and still keeps their broker in prison. His covetousness has made him generally hated. (<em>Copy.</em> 3¾ <em>pp. Received March</em> 28.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">PRESIDENT METHWOLD AND COUNCIL AT SURAT TO THE FACTORS AT BARODA, MARCH 23, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 99).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send a parwāna from the Governor of Surat for the clearing of their goods at Broach. Will settle later as to a bribe for the Customer. Purchase of certain cloth. The <em>Discovery</em> arrived on the 17th. No duties should be paid at Ankleswar or elsewhere on the route. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THE SAME TO THE FACTORS AT AHMADĀBĀD, MARCH 24, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 100).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Michael</em> will not sail before April 1, and so the indigo is likely to be down in time. Wish that the rest and the saltpetre were well on the road. The <em>Kit</em> is not yet back from Cambay. As for the business of the brokers, ‘what you must be forced to pay, doe it without clamour. A new broker need not be appointed yet awhile. News from Persia brought by the <em>Discovery</em>. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">BENJAMIN ROBINSON AND EDWARD ABBOT AT AHMADĀBĀD TO THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT, MARCH 25, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 596).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Hear that the <em>Kit</em> has left Cambay. The trouble with the brokers. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp. Received March</em> 30.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">ABEL DRUCE AND JOSEPH DOWNHAM AT ‘CARROLE’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-039" href="#sec-06-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> TO THE SAME, MARCH 27, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 597).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">With the aid of the parwāna, got their goods ‘customed’ and over the river. Hear nothing of ‘the Banian Drew’,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-040" href="#sec-06-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> but there is another knave in his place, who takes the same course. Payment to ‘Tulcidas’, who is in trouble with his creditors. Hope to reach ‘Raneale’ to-morrow. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp. Received March</em> 30.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-231"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="231" data-folio="185" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n230_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 185">[p. 185]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold at Surat to the English Consul at Aleppo</span>,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-041" href="#sec-06-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">March 28, 1636</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 102).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests him to transmit to the Company the enclosed letters, which are sent by a new route, viz. via Basrā. Reminds him of their former acquaintance in England. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Instructions from President Methwold and Council to John Drake, March 28, 1636</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 443).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To proceed in the pinnace <em>Michael</em> to Basrā, with Joseph Downham as his assistant. The goods on board are shown in the invoice; and the accompanying list (procured from the Captain of Damān) gives the prices current at Basrā last year, which will be a rough guide. What weight is current there they cannot learn, ‘nor more of the mony then that they are larrees, wherein you may bee deceived, since (as wee heare) they have of late much abased them in their alloy.’ Have written to the ‘Bashaw’ intimating the great amity that exists between the King of England and the ‘Emperour of Turkie’; and have also sent him a valuable present of Indian stuffs. ‘If wee may beleive the relacion of one of his owne people now lately departed from hence, you wilbe exceedeingly wellcome unto him, that hath longe desired to have commerce with our nacion. For the conditions of our trade, wee may not expect better or other then those whereunto the Portugalls doe submitt, and withall you may not willingly consent unto worse. Wee doe not certainely know what customes they pay.’ Have written to ‘Padree Frero Basilio, a Carmelite who hath lived many years in that place’, begging his assistance; at the delivery of the letter a present should be given him. Have also addressed a letter to the ‘Captaine Major of the Portugalls, who hath the charge of those straights,’ assuring him that the pinnace has no goods on board ‘apperteyneing to any Moores or heathens, whereby the revenewes of the port of Muscatt should bee any wayes defrauded.’ If necessary, he may be shown the invoice, ‘the steele alone omitted, for that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-232" data-page="232" data-folio="186" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n231_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 186">[p. 186]</button> is a prohibitted comodety, as wee have since understood.' A suitable present should be offered to him. During the voyage, and also at Basrā, precautions should be taken against treachery; but it is hoped that ‘you shall have to doe with civill and rationall men, though Mahumetans’, who will not do anything to damage the trade of their port. Letters for the Company are herewith delivered to him, to be forwarded (with his own) to Aleppo by means of ‘cassetts [couriers: Arabic <em>qāsid</em>] of that country’; and duplicates should be sent by a separate conveyance, as it is important that the Company should receive intelligence of the dispatch of the <em>Discovery</em>. To return to Surat some time in September. While waiting, the <em>Michael</em> may be employed to ‘Catiffe’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-042" href="#sec-06-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> or some other place in Persia or Arabia, provided that this can be done without giving offence to the Portuguese. If ‘larrees’ be taken in payment, these should be changed into rials of eight, ‘Venetians’, ‘Ebrahins’,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-043" href="#sec-06-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> etc. Young horses from thence are of very great esteem in India, and some may be brought if they can be got reasonably; also a supply of dates. As regards pearls, trial may be made both of the round sort and such as are called ‘kitchery’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-044" href="#sec-06-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>; the latter are sold by ‘the mitscall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-045" href="#sec-06-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> (which is the waight of a larree) for 6, 7, and more larrees’. Should the <em>Michael</em> return empty, or only partially filled, a call may be made at Muskat, to offer to transport any horses, etc., which the Captain there may wish to send to Goa or elsewhere. (<em>Copy.</em> 6 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Commission and Instructions to Thomas Byam, Master of the</span> <em>Michael</em>, <span class="sc">March</span> 28, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 449).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A pilot should be procured at the island of Larak or at Kangūn, and another at the ‘Ila de Carga’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-046" href="#sec-06-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>. While at Basrā he is to take special care of the behaviour of his crew. The interim employment of the <em>Michael</em> is to be settled with Drake. Private trade to be prevented. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-233"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="233" data-folio="187" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n232_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 187">[p. 187]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3">PRESIDENT METHWOLD AND COUNCIL AT SURAT TO JOHN WHITE [AT SWALLY], MARCH 29, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 103).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Bassein</em> is to be fitted out to carry horses and carpets to the Captain of Damān and bring back some arrack. Will come down shortly to dispeed the <em>Michael</em>. (<em>Copy</em>. ¾ <em>p</em>.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THOMAS BYAM, ABOARD THE <em>MICHAEL</em> IN SWALLY ROAD, TO THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT, MARCH 30, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 595).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requesting a supply of medicine and coloured calico. (<em>Copy</em>. ¼ <em>p</em>.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">WILLIAM GIBSON AND JOHN WILLOUGHBY, ABOARD THE <em>WILLIAM</em> AT GOMBROON, TO THE SAME, MARCH 30, 1636 (<em>Ibid</em>., p. 617).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">If a ship be sent to Gombroon from Surat, she should be laden with sugar, sugar candy, coffee, gum-lac, butter, pepper, rice, lamp-oil, cotton wool, and indigo. These commodities they could vend to the value of 5,000 or 6,000 tūmāns yearly. The Dutch have sold spices at this port to the value of upwards of 10,000 tūmāns annually for the last three years; but it would not pay to buy such goods at Surat (pepper excepted) for this purpose. Arrival of the <em>William</em> on March 13. Answer various points in the letter from Surat. Describe the devices used to defraud the English of their share of the customs. Suggest that before a pass is issued to any vessel bound for Gombroon, a list of the cargo should be required, which should then be forwarded to them. Approve the dispatch of a vessel to Basrā; it is said, however, that prices there depend much upon the arrival of caravans. Cannot send the required accounts, owing to the death of one accountant after another, and the illness of Mr. Griffith, who has them now in charge. Will endeavour to remit by exchange the 500 tūmāns received for freight of the goods in the <em>William</em>. A letter arrived from Masulipatam on March 18 by the junk which used to belong to Mīr Kamāldīn, but is now the property of the King of Golconda. She carried English colours and was ‘guarded by six<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-047" href="#sec-06-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of our English’. Four more Dutch <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-234" data-page="234" data-folio="188" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n233_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 188">[p. 188]</button> ships arrived on March 20 under the command of ‘Batack’ [Jacob Janszoon Patacca]. They are endeavouring to ‘gett away our fraught from us’, but with scant success; so they are now gone to Ormus to lade stones for ballast. Have just heard of the death of Thomas Griffith at Lar. The ‘semiano’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-048" href="#sec-06-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> formerly sent for Willoughby’s use is grown old and rotten; desire that another may be sent. Find that they cannot get bills of exchange, as intended. Deficiency in abbāsīs sent to Masulipatam. Two horses dispatched thither; there was not room for more. Dispute as to a bale of sugar sold by the surgeon of the <em>William</em>.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-049" href="#sec-06-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>Copy. 8 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> pp. Received May 7.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Consultation held in Surat by President Methwold and Messrs. Breton, Peirson, Bornford, Wills, White, Lucas, and Williamson, April</span> 1, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 452).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is decided that the <em>Discovery</em> shall be the ship to return to England. Provisions for the voyage. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Benjamin Robinson and Edward Abbot at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, April</span> 2, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. cii. p. 598).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The ‘puttolaes’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-050" href="#sec-06-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> have been sent by the Dutch caravan. No long beads could be procured at Cambay. Have been ordered by the Dīwān to pay part of the money claimed by ‘Panjew’. A broker much needed; suggest the appointment of ‘Midas’ [Mahī Dās]. The Dutch declare that they will have procured in all 1,200 bales of Persian silk this season; wish the Company had so good a quantity. (<em>Copy. 1 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> pp. Received April 8.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-235"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="235" data-folio="189" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n234_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 189">[p. 189]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 4, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 600).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The enclosed letters arrived yesterday from Fremlen at ‘Bandur Laree’. Send also letters from ‘Dongee’ at Agra, notifying the receipt of their bills of exchange. The Dutch, having sold their cloves, etc., are buying all the cotton yarn they can get and are inquiring for indigo. Some broadcloth wanted. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received April</em> 10.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold and Council at Surat to the Agent and Factors in Persia</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 4, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 103).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Reply to their letter brought by the <em>Discovery</em>. The <em>Hopewell</em> has not yet returned. Disposal of the Masulipatam goods and their proceeds. Justify their former censure on the backwardness of the Persia accounts. Seven horses received by the <em>Discovery</em>; would be glad of more, if possible in better condition. Remarks on the new contract with the Shāh. The Governor of Surat has lately been changed. The newcomer they conceive to be a better man than ‘Merza Mulke’, though most people complain that he is a worse Governor. Detail the farmān he recently procured concerning the English and the Dutch. He pretends it is a mere form; but they have shown their resentment by abstaining from the usual visits. He at first refused to permit the President to go on board the <em>Discovery</em>, but consented on learning that the native passengers would be detained in consequence. Intend to get the farmān abrogated at court. Will not insist upon a charge being made for wrappers etc., though the rice bags cost a mahmūdī apiece. It does not matter whether they buy the copper or not. Civilities shown to certain Portuguese. Suggest the purchase of some of the mace and nutmegs brought by the Dutch; but this must be done through an agent, as the Dutch are forbidden by the Council of Batavia to sell these spices to the English. Money found to be missing from the bags. The bales of silk should be numbered, to obviate their being stolen on the road. Thank them for the particulars concerning Rastell’s estate.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-051" href="#sec-06-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> The <em>London</em> arrived on March 31, but <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-236" data-page="236" data-folio="190" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n235_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 190">[p. 190]</button> they are still awaiting the <em>Hopewell</em> before dispatching the <em>Discovery</em>. Letters to be transmitted to the Company. (<em>Copy.</em> 8 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Gibson and John Willoughby at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, April 8, 1636</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 627).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send copies of the letters dispatched by the <em>William</em>. Now answer the Surat postscript of February 4. The lead has all been sold long since. Have paid special care to the baggage of the passengers in that vessel. Hope to include their tin in the next contract at a remunerative price. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received by a Surat junk on May 8.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Methwold and George Peirson at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, April 8, 1636</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 112).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrival of the <em>London</em> and the <em>Hopewell</em>; also of the indigo and saltpetre caravan. Clamour in Surat on account of the robbery of some junks by an English ship,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-052" href="#sec-06-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> the captain of which is said to be 'the kinsman of the captain in Agra'. A guard has been placed in the factory, and the caravan has been stopped. Payment has been claimed of losses pretended to amount to 100,000 rupees; but they intend to resist this. (<em>Copy.</em> 2¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Consultation held aboard the</span> <em>London</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>] <span class="sc">by Messrs. Fremlen, Breton, Bornford, Drake, Wills, White, and Godfrey, April 8, 1636</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 453).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">President Methwold has been confined to his chamber in Surat, with a guard of fifty soldiers on the house, owing to his refusal to pay a large sum which the Governor and merchants allege they have lost in three [<em>sic</em>] junks supposed to have been taken by English ships. It is therefore resolved: (1) that the guard over the goods on the Marine be increased, in order to secure them while water and provisions are taken on board; (2) that, as the <em>Hopewell</em> is suspected of the robbery, the commander and crew shall be strictly examined; (3) that the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Kit</em> shall go down to the mouth of the Surat River and there seize all junks attempting <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-237" data-page="237" data-folio="191" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n236_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 191">[p. 191]</button> to go in or out, in order that the crews may be examined and 'this scandalous report to the disparadgement of the nation may appeare out of their owne mouthes nothinge but fixions'; (4) that, on the arrival of the <em>William</em> from Persia, Capt. Brown shall be instructed to detain all passengers and their goods until further orders. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">President Methwold and George Peirson at Surat</span>] <span class="sc">to [the Factors] aboard the</span> <em>London</em> [<span class="sc">April</span> 9 <span class="sc">or</span> 10, 1636] (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. p. 114).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Relate their negotiations with the Governor and others, and their present confinement in a prison just vacated by 'Nonavassie' [Nānā Vaisya], who 'hath left soe many of his attendance (besides his guard) that wee can hardlie sleepe for chintches' [bugs: Sp. <em>chinche</em>]. The guard has been withdrawn from the factory, which is now quiet, save for eight sailors, who have been 'roaguing about the towne untill famine brought them in'. Are 'nothing dejected', but beseech the factors to consult frequently of the most suitable 'wayes of reparation', and above all to consider the necessities of the Company's affairs. (<em>The rest is missing. Copy.</em> 2¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Messrs. Fremlen, Breton and others] aboard the</span> <em>London</em> <span class="sc">at Swally to the Company, April</span> 12, 1636 (<em>O.C.</em> 1556).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The return of the <em>Michael</em>, owing to her springing a leak soon after sailing, has enabled them to make an addition to the letter sent by her. Arrival of the <em>Hopewell</em> [from Lārībandar] on April 3, bringing 79 bales of piece-goods, one of indigo, and 60 of cotton yarn; also a quantity of 'arseffætida', 'purwasse', 'googur', and 'cambeera', these being usually brought thence by native merchants. The country yields all sorts of commodities vendible in England, Persia, and Turkey; 'more espetiallie the latter, whether the cheifest parte of goods procureable in Synda are yeerelie laden. Those, seasonably invested, doe doubtlesse retorne their owners good proffitt; for of all sorts of Indian goods none are in such request as those of Synda nor finde more reddie vend, as being in reguarde of their substance and coullers most requireable. For the roade, it is far from shore, and a most daungerous barre interplaced, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-238" data-page="238" data-folio="192" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n237_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 192">[p. 192]</button> denieing passage to ships that drawe above 12 foote water in the easterlie monzone, and in the westerly 10 or 11 foote, for then the winds off the sea are most violent and, meeting with that strong outlett of the river, raiseth the sands upon the barre and maketh one or two foote differrence in depth of water thereon. Notwithstanding from the beginning October to the middle February the land winds keepe the barre so smooth that shipps of what burthen soever may imbarque their goods with as much conveniency as may bee desired; and being fitted with vessells of such a drought as mencioned, namelie 9, 10, or 11 foote water, may freely from the fine [i. e. end] September to the fine March goe and retorne at their pleasure. For watering and woodding nature hath provided abundantly and conveniently gratis to all such as please to make use of it. Provisions of all sorts are there most plentifullie to be acquierd, and cheaper by farre then any other place of India at present affoards them. Besides these, the accustomary manner of conveyance of goods bought in Agra by Synda marchants, being first transported to Lahoare or Multan on carts and thence by river to their place of embarquing, not comeing to cost them above three rup[ee]s per m[aun]d of 72 lb., is considerable, whenas your goods bought in Agra, besides the daungerous and tedious passage they have by Amadabad or Brampore, heape up to their prime cost such unavoydable and excessive charges that it cannot but cause admiracion in all such as behould it.’ On Fremlen’s departure he left the remaining goods and money (nearly 10,000 rupees) in the hands of the brokers, to be invested in calicoes. For the alleged piracy committed by some English vessel and the consequent imprisonment of the President, they refer to his own letters and to the copy of one written to Mīrzā Mahmūd by the master of his junk [<em>see</em> p. 197]. The lateness of the season hinders them from taking any measures to secure redress, for, although it was decided to send the <em>Blessing</em> to blockade the river, the state of the weather has prevented this. Certain goods from Ahmadābād and Agra have been stopped, and the ships are in need of water and provisions. Unless the Ahmadābād goods are released, the <em>Discovery</em> must sail partly empty; and in that case they may send her to Bantam on her way home. The rest of the ships will be disposed of in the manner first intended; but they will be detained until the 20th current, in the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-239" data-page="239" data-folio="193" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories05fostuoft/page/n238_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 193">[p. 193]</button> hope of ‘enforcing this people to some more reasonable composicion’ by seizing their junks, and also in expectation of the arrival of the <em>William</em>. If provisions are not then available, they must rely upon procuring a supply at Damān, Dābhol, or Goa. (<em>Copy, unsigned. 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Pitt at Dābhol to the President and Council at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 15, 1636 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 604).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No goods should be sent here, for yesterday came news that the Mogul with his army was within three days of this place. Alarm in the town. If any English ships come, he will embark his goods and go to [Goa]. Hopes to buy some pepper. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received April</em> 23.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, April</span> 17, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 615).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>William</em> arrived yesterday from Persia, laden with freight-goods, mostly for Masulipatam, whither she will proceed after landing the portion of her cargo consigned to this place. The King’s farmān is expected daily. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received May</em> 7.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Gilson and Thomas Wilbraham, aboard the <em>William</em> at Dābhol, to the Same, April</span> 17, 1636 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 616).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Owing to contrary winds, the ship did not reach Gombroon until March 13. Difficulty in obtaining freight, as the native merchants resent the customs duties extorted at Surat. Found in the road the three Dutch ships which left Swally on January 14; the other four arrived on March 20. Rice borrowed from them. On March 16 came in the vessel belonging to the Governor of Surat; she had been forced by a storm to throw overboard great part of her cargo.
