Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Stated in the text to be September 27, 1635, but the right date appears to be the 5th of that month.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Another list, written in the margin of p. 143 of <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii, gives the captain’s name as ‘Gooliergoo’, adding that he has ‘one tooth before’, that he has a brother Thomas, and a boy Tom: the mate is ‘John’ [i. e. Jones]: ‘Frangely’ is purser: ‘Dawle’ surgeon: ‘Cooper’ steward; and ‘John Chup’ carpenter, with a mate named Sam and a boy named Harry. It is also stated that the crew wore English clothes, and that the flag was carried in the foretop. Among the <em>Domestic State Papers</em> (vol. cclxxxiv. no. 56) is a list of twenty-two men belonging to the <em>Samaritan</em> and <em>Roebuck</em>; but the only one who can be identified as occurring also in the above list is Thomas Ayres, who was evidently the captain’s brother. Of the other names ‘Frangely’ is probably Franklin, who is mentioned in the Court Minutes for May 24, 1644, as having been on board, together with a surgeon named Glover. Apparently the <em>Roebuck’s</em> consort was always alluded to as the <em>William</em> (see the Dutch account in <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 328). This naturally increased the suspicion that the piracy had been committed by the missing <em>Crispiana</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">At the end of the copy in the diary Methwold notes that ‘it is considerable allso that, notwithstanding their most inhuman usadge of the people belonging to the shippe of Dio, they did noe violence to those of Surratt, but left aboord them certaine chists of corrall, saffron, coho seede [coffee berries], and some rohonas; and to a certaine churchman called Cogee Hobullhosyne [Khwāja Abūl Husain] they returned againe 200 venecians which they had taken from him.’ Cf. p. 215, where the money is said to have been 200 ibrāhīmīs.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For another copy see the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>. The fact that Methwold endeavoured to throw the blame of the piracy on the French is mentioned in the Dutch records (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 112).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A Dutch letter of Dec. <sup>4</sup>/<sub>14</sub>, 1634 (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 320) states that the English fleet had met two French vessels which had been in the Red Sea and had taken a small ship. It goes on to say that Mīr Mūsā asked the Dutch to treat the Frenchmen as pirates, should they meet them, but they returned an evasive answer.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hague Transcripts</em> (<em>ibid.</em>, no. 333). By ‘Barin’, Barent Pieterszoon is probably meant.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a contemporary copy (dated May 1) in <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. (p. 125); and another (dated as above) among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Fr. <em>quart d'écu</em>, a silver coin worth about 1<em>s</em>. 6<em>d</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 52.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">John Tradescant the elder, the noted naturalist and curiosity-hunter, whose collection, bequeathed by his son to Elias Ashmole, became the nucleus of the present Ashmolean Museum. Several Indian curiosities, including ‘a sowes head from Surat’ are enumerated in the early catalogue. The following extract from the Court Minutes of November 27, 1633, will explain the reference in the text:—‘A letter was nowe read from Mr. Secretary Windebanke, signifying His Majesties pleasure that the Company should write for such rarities as are expressed in a paper thereinclosed, and beeing returned to deliver them to John Tredescant to be reserved by him for His Majesties service. The Court, in obedience thereunto, delivered the said paper to Mr. Ellam and required him to have care thereof and to send the same by their next shipps to their severall factories, that such things as may bee had may bee returned for England according to His Majesties pleasure.’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Champa, the Malay name for a part of Indo-China. Fryer says (p. 116): ‘From Siam are brought hither [Surat] little Champore cocks with ruffed feet, well armed <span class="folio-turn">[p. 208]</span> with spurs, which have a strutting gate with them, the truest mettled in the world; they are generally white, with an eye of yellow.’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mandelslo noticed that both Methwold and Fremlen abstained from taking the heavy supper in which the rest of the English indulged.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">William Spurstow, one of the Committees. The reference seems to be to some coral which he had been permitted to send to India for sale on his own account (see <em>Court Minutes</em>, 1635-39, p. 11).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Rāybāg, in Kolhāpur State, about 200 miles south-south-east of Dābhol.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Two contemporary copies of the following narrative will be found in nos. 28 ii and 28 iii of vol. vi of the <em>C.O.</em> 77 series at the Public Record Office.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The duplicate has ‘Saincts’ (for ‘saints’?). The meaning appears to be that the only satisfaction the wronged parties were likely to obtain was that of bearing with saintlike meekness an unmerited injury.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cf. p. 201. 'Seid' is the Arabic <em>saiyid</em> ('a lord'), the title used in India for those who claim descent from Muhammad.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See The Court Minutes of the East India Company, 1635–39</em>, p. 217.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He had been taken out to Persia by William Hoare, apparently without the Company's permission (<em>O.C.</em> 1538).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The lading consisted chiefly of indigo, saltpetre, cotton yarn, Persian raw silk, and some Chinese silk stuffs brought from Macao in the <em>London</em> (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 247).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently ‘Flemish shillings’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Portuguese failed to intercept the Dutch ships: see the <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. nos. 324, 327, 333, and the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, pp. 250, 273; also the <em>Lisbon Transcripts, Doc. Remett.</em>, book 37, f. 531.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a list of accounts, wills, transcripts of correspondence, etc., sent home with this letter see <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part iii. pp. 156, 157.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mulher, in Bāglān: see the 1618–21 volume, p. 91, and the 1622–23 volume, pp. xl, 25.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Peyton (Brit. Mus. <em>Addl. MS.</em> 19,276) distinguishes between the old rials of eight with a plain cross (which he says were reckoned the better) and the new ones with fleurs-de-lys at the ends of the cross.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The prophecy referred to is doubtless that mentioned in a letter from Bantam, dated January 19, 1618 (<em>O.C.</em> 595):— ‘Yt was revealed by orackle unto the Emperour [of China] that his countrey should be subdued by a gray eyed people, and doe therfore forbidd all Christians his countrey.’ In a representation from the city of Macao to King Charles on the subject of Weddell’s voyage (<em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 41, f. 220), it is stated that the Chinese <span class="folio-turn">[p. 227]</span> objected to the coming of the English because they had blue eyes and would (it was feared) take the kingdom from its present possessors.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Later in the letter Bornford includes among the China goods vendible in Goa ‘gold, the 10 teen att 195 rials’; and from this it is clear that by ‘teen’ he means the tael or Chinese ounce (cf. Peter Mundy’s account of China, in which he speaks of ‘one loaf of gold, poiz 10 taes’). Fryer (<em>New Account</em>, p. 210) also uses ‘teen’ in this sense, in speaking of the currency of Macao. I cannot find any other instance, but it seems probable from these examples that ‘teen’ was generally used by the Portuguese at Macao for ‘tael’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Lisbon Transcripts</em>, <em>Doc. Remett.</em>, book 35, ff. 261, 267.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Lisbon Transcripts</em>, <em>Doc. Remett.</em>, book 34, ff. 5, 41.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pachak, or costus root, obtained from Kashmīr and much used in China for incense.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Storax (Sanskrit <em>rasa-mālā</em>, Hind. <em>silā-ras</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably catechu (Hind. <em>katthā</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The origin of this term has not been discovered, unless it be connected with the obsolete English word <em>auge</em>, ‘to increase.’ From other instances of its use, it seems to mean money lent to a merchant to enable him to purchase goods for shipment, repayable (with interest) to the lender's nominee at the place to which the goods have been consigned.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">As stated on p. 159, this is practically an excerpt from Methwold's diary. For other contemporary copies of the narrative see no. 28 i of vol. vi of the <em>C.O.</em> 77 series at the Public Record Office, and the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em> and <em>Triplicates</em> at the India Office.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ichhāpur, about five miles west of Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Dīwān</em>. Later, Methwold terms him Amīr Beg. In the Dutch account (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 328) he is called 'Amerbeecq Amyn', i. e. the <em>Amīn</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This incident is related in the <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 337, but nothing of importance is added. For Venant see a note on p. 59.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">We know of course that W. A. was William Ayres, the master of the <em>Roebuck</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Further copies will be found in <em>O.C.</em> 1548 (and duplicate) and nos. 28 ii, 28 iii in vol. vi of the <em>C.O.</em> 77 series at the Public Record Office. There is a Dutch version in <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 337, and in the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 249. In this the date is given as September 22. Neither date fits in with the <em>nākhudā’s</em> narrative.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Similarly the Viceroy of Goa wrote of Shāh Jahān as ‘the declared enemy of the Christian name’ (<em>Lisbon Transcripts, Doc. Remett.</em>, book 33, f. 258).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Among the <em>Hague Transcripts</em> (series i. vol. x. no. 336) is a Dutch translation of a letter from Methwold to Barent Pieterszoon, the Dutch chief, dated April 12, expressing astonishment that none of the Hollanders had either written to or visited him. Methwold goes on to say that he hears that they are really encouraging the Governor and have promised to protect the Indian vessels against the English; but he can hardly believe this of them. The letter is printed at p. 114 of the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636. In the same volume of the <em>Transcripts</em> (no. 328) will be found Pieterszoon’s letter to Batavia, detailing the events leading up to Methwold’s imprisonment. He represents himself as preserving a neutral attitude. Mention is made of a letter from the Governor to Batavia, but the contents are not indicated. See also p. 112 of the <em>Dagh-Register</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the Court Minutes for April 3, 1626.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Other copies will be found among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em> and <em>Triplicates</em>, and at p. 457 of <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the <em>Reformation</em> should be substituted for the <em>Palsgrave</em>. The voyage referred to was in 1630: see the last volume, p. 41. The island was Réunion.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The rest of the journal is occupied with the homeward voyage of the <em>London</em> and <em>Jewel</em> from Bantam (January–July, 1637).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 155.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Persian <em>nazar-band</em>, ‘strictly watched,’ ‘under surveillance.’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to the Dutch records (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 247) the Governor put the consequent loss to the Surat customs at 50,000 rupees.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A slip for 117,000 (see p. 257).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">These are not extant, but according to the Dutch Methwold denied liability, as the robbery had been committed by an English rover, not by the Company’s servants (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 248); and this is confirmed by the Portuguese records (<em>Lisbon Transcripts, Doc. Remett.</em>, book 37, f. 429).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The claim, which was for 3,450 rials (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 271) was afterwards admitted (see p. 272).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Milaks</em> are mentioned in the <em>Āīn</em> (vol. i. p. 92) as gold stuffs from Persia.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>qutnī</em>: see the 1618–21 volume, pp. 10, 92.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 272.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Prince Aurangzīb was appointed to the government of the Deccan in April, 1636 (Elliot and Dowson’s <em>History</em>, vol. vii. p. 58).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Dutch say that it arrived on the 3rd. According to them it made no allusion to the robbery of the Diu junk, but ordered Methwold's release as soon as the Surat merchants were compensated. He was, however, to be watched to see that he did not leave the city, and the English were not to be allowed to trade with Tatta or Cambay. (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 246.)</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīr Muhammad Bāqir, who had been given the title of Irādat Khān in 1606, and that of Āzim Khān in 1630. Under Shāh Jahān he had taken a prominent part in the Deccan campaigns, and was now appointed Viceroy of Gujarāt in order to put down the depredations of the Kolīs and other turbulent tribes. In 1637 he built a splendid palace at Ahmadābād, now used as a jail. An interesting account of him is given by Mandelslo, who paid him two visits during his stay at Ahmadābād in the autumn of 1638.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Irādat Khān, i. e. Āzim Khān (see p. 257).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the introduction to the 1630-33 volume, p. vi.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The general belief in lucky and unlucky days is noted by most travellers of the period.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly <em>korā</em> (newly coined) rupees : see the 1618–21 volume, p. 113.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kharkī or Kirkī, i. e. Aurangābād, which was founded in 1610 by Malik Ambar. The present name was given to it by Aurangzīb.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>hūn</em>, or gold pagoda. The amount was exaggerated. The <em>Bādshāhnāma</em> (Elliot and Dowson’s <em>History</em>, vol. vii. p. 57) says it was the equivalent of twenty lakhs [of rupees] in jewels, elephants, etc. The Dutch mention a report that the agreement was for fifty lakhs of rupees (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 273).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A Dutchman named Cornelis Janszoon Silvius.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The name of the author of this narrative is not given, but he must have been either one of the officers of the <em>Swan</em>, or Robert Phipps, who went out in that ship as a factor. The second part is in a different hand.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">From this it appears that Proud went ashore with his boats and parleyed with the men of the <em>Roebuck</em>, pointing out the danger of their being robbed by the islanders. 'Their answeare was that from the country people or the King of Mumbas [Mombasa] (which then lay very neare unto them with three hundred men) they were able to secure or defend themselves from them, and for our parts if we came for anything there itt should bee upon the perrill of our lives.' For the King of Mombasa see Faria y Sousa's <em>Asia Portuguesa</em>, vol. iii. pp. 476, 506. After abandoning that city he took refuge in Arabia and later on proceeded to Madagascar. Probably he was at this time on his way to the latter island.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is entered in full. It is dated February 27, 1635, and authorizes Cobb with his two ships ‘to range the seas all the wourld over’, especially from the Cape to China and Japan, including the Red Sea, Persian Gulf, and Coromandel Coast, and ‘to make prize of all such the treasures, merchandizes, goodes, and commodities which to his best abillities hee shall be able to take of infidells or of anie other prince, potentat, or state not in league or amitie with Us beyond the lyne equinoctiall, notwithstanding our former commission graunted to our lyall subject Richard Ofield, master of the said shipp [<em>Samaritan</em>], bearing date the 25 day of this present month, for to range and discover from Cape [<em>blank</em>] in Virginia to Cape Florida in Nova Spania and all others the rivers and coast of America.’ In the event of Cobb’s death, Ofield is to succeed him; and should both die, a fresh commission is to be opened. In the voyage such instructions are to be followed as shall be given by Endymion Porter, Thomas ‘Kenistone’ [Kynaston], and Samuel Bonnell; to them account is to be rendered of all captures, and they will be responsible for the wages or shares of the crews. When returning Cobb may, if necessary, claim aid or convoy from any navy or merchant ships. Both vessels are authorized to wear ‘our collors appoynted for our royall navie’. All neighbouring princes and allies are entreated, and all English subjects are commanded, to assist Cobb in anything he may require. After the commission is entered a copy of a royal letter of the same date, addressed to the Presidents at Surat and Bantam, the Agent in Persia, Captains Weddell and Brown, and any other servants of the East India Company, commanding them to aid and assist Cobb or his successor with munitions, provisions, or men, upon his giving satisfaction for the same.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is entered at full length. To the account already given, it adds that Jones offered to deliver the money to Cobb, if the latter would come on shore for that purpose ; and that on being told by Proud that he would be carried to Surat to answer for what he had done, Jones replied that ‘he should first see the shipp blowne upp, and that it should cost hott water to fetch him’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An agreement between Proud and Cobb to this effect will be found in <em>O.C.</em> 1574. It recites the facts, and explains that Jones was accepted as a substitute for Ayres because the latter was nominated in the royal commission to succeed Cobb, and his removal might ‘bee detriment unto His Majesties further designes’. Cobb also acknowledges that he has requisitioned some supplies from the <em>Swan</em> by virtue of the royal commission.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kalyānjī (see p. 264). For the Dutch complaints see the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 272.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>chavana</em>, ‘a tea or coffee cup’. The latter is here meant, for Methwold, in referring to the matter later, expressly said that the missing articles were ‘coho-dishes’ (<em>Court Minutes, 1640–43</em>, p. 40).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The head (<em>sar</em>) of the district (<em>samt</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably at Akbarpur, where the road from Burhānpur to Mālndū crossed the Narbadā.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For Bengal (see the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 274).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Tāptī, on which Burhānpur is situated.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-137" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 245.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Entered on the same page. It notifies the dispatch of the <em>Dragon</em>, <em>Sun</em>, <em>Katherine</em>, <em>Planter</em>, <em>Anne</em>, and <em>Discovery</em> ‘by our spetiall command for a voyadge and discovery to the South Sea . . . in which adventure Wee have a particular interest’, and orders them to be supplied with provisions and other necessaries and to be permitted to proceed in their voyage without hindrance. It is addressed to the ‘President and Councell of Our East India Company residing in the Indies’, and is dated March 30, 1636. Appended is a certificate by William Bayley and William Slade that the copy is a true one.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-140" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-140" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Undated, but evidently written between April 8, 1636 (when the previous letter was sent) and early in the following September, when Gibson started for court (<em>O.C.</em> 1575).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-141" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-141" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The concluding portion of this last letter will be found at p. 639 of the volume, but it is in such a damaged state that it is practically illegible.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-142" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-142" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bālāghāt, the country ‘above the passes’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-143" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-143" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Agharī, about four miles north-west of Dābhol.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-144" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-144" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Chiplūn, which is about 25 miles up the Vāshishtī River, near the head of the Kumbhārlī pass, one of the easiest routes from the Deccan to the seaboard.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-145" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">1 · [Unlinked note, p. 288]</strong> <span class="fn-body">Mahr. <em>desāī</em>, an official in charge of a district. For the discontent excited by Masīh-uz-Zamān's administration see a Dutch letter from Surat summarized in the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 271.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-146" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-146" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jāfar Khān, nephew and son-in-law of Āsaf Khān. He was one of the chief men at court, and later on became Wazīr to Aurangzīb.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-147" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-147" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For trade in Bengal. A translation is given in the <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. x. no. 328, and in the <em>Dagh-Register</em> for 1637, p. 105 (reprinted in Heeres’s <em>Corpus Diplomaticum</em>, p. 280). If the translation is correct, Drake was misinformed, as no mention is made of the English.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-148" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-148" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Java galangal, a rhizome much used in Indian medicine. Linschoten terms it ‘ lanquas ’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-149" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-149" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This seems to be the Mahratti <em>sajganī</em>, the strict meaning of which is ‘a couple of pice’. Probably in this connexion ordinary pice are intended.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-150" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-150" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">In a later letter (<em>O.C.</em> 1740) this is called ‘bunnah’ and described as an inferior sort of indigo, yet still of fair quality.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-151" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-151" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Speare (p. 251) says the 6th, but probably he is wrong.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Speare (p. 251) and Lee (p. 283) say the 14th; while the <em>Dagh-Register, 1636</em> (p. 268) gives the date as the 16th.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>chakar</em>, a roll or bale. Elsewhere it is stated to consist of <em>four</em> pieces (<em>Letters Received</em>, vol. iv. p. 239, vol. vi. p. 236). For <em>guldārīs</em> and <em>Farhat Khānīs</em> see <em>supra</em>, p. 219, and the 1624-29 volume, p. 63.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>St. Jacob</em> and the <em>St. Anna</em> (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 269).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-155" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-155" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 155.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-156" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-156" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to Franco’s <em>Synopsis Annalium S. J.</em>, Stanislaus Malpica, an Italian by birth, left Lisbon for the East in 1635.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-157" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-157" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Representations were afterwards made from Goa on the same subject: see <em>Lisbon Transcripts, Doc. Remett.</em>, vol. x. book 37, f. 429.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-158" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-158" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was appointed master of the <em>Mary</em> on February 10, 1636, having previously been a mate on board the <em>Hart</em>. For consultations held during the voyage see <em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 16.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-159" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-159" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The boat had been driven on the rocks by the wind and had gone to pieces, with the result that Price and seven others were drowned. The four survivors were picked up by the Dutch <em>Zutphen</em>, which arrived on August 4, and were carried by her to Batavia (see <em>O.C.</em> 1588 and <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, pp. 261, 275).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-160" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-160" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See Court Minutes of the East India Company, 1635–9</em>, p. 162.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-161" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-161" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Olearius's account of the Holstein mission (English edition of 1662, p. 268).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-162" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-162" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Undated. The narrative is signed also by Philip Harris (coxswain), William Watson (interpreter), and Henry Jenkinson, but it was evidently written by Brown.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-163" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-163" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was of course incorrect.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-164" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-164" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>The Journal of John Jourdain</em>, p. 140, note 2.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-165" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-165" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For another account of the proceedings, see the consultations mentioned on p. 305 <em>n.</em></span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-166" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-166" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the copyist’s error for ‘prop’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-167" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-167" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The copyist’s error for ‘in their’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-168" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-168" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Court appears to have been unaware that Armagon was outside the authority of the King of Golconda.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-169" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-169" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is the original letter, but it bears no date. It was probably written, like the preceding document, at the end of November. A copy (also undated) will be found at f. 223 of <em>Rawlinson MS.</em> A 299 in the Bodleian Library.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-170" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-170" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Porto Novo is on the Madras seaboard, between Cuddalore and Tranquebar. The name was given by the Portuguese, who are said to have established a settlement there in the latter part of the sixteenth century. The place was mentioned in the 1624–29 volume (p. 14), but the present is the earliest reference to any English trade there.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-171" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-171" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The post of Sar-i-Khail (Lt.-Col. T. W. Haig tells me) was that of commander of the royal bodyguard, but the official thus designated at Golconda seems to have been in charge of the revenues. In a letter of 1639 he is spoken of as 'cheife governor under the King'. When the King of Golconda visited the English ships at Masulipatam in 1676 the 'Cirkell' was with him, and the title was then explained as 'chancellor'. See also the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1631–34, p. 45, where the word is spelt 'Sarchijl'.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-172" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-172" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>maulavī</em>, a judge, doctor of the law, etc.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-173" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-173" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Give a present (Pers. <em>pīshkash</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-174" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-174" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Carel Renierszoon. See the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 268.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-175" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-175" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">In a marginal note these sums are converted at 2<em>s.</em> 6<em>d.</em> to the rupee, and 10<em>s.</em> to the pagoda.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-176" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-176" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Barent Pessaert (see the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1636, p. 269, and 1637, p. 94). He was Dutch by birth and had formerly been in the service of the Dutch East India Company ; but, having quarrelled with his superiors, he entered the employment of the Danish Company.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-177" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-177" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy among the <em>O.C.</em> duplicates.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-178" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-178" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Signed by Weddell, Nathaniel and John Mountney, and Thomas Robinson. For another account of the outward voyage see the MS. journal of Peter Mundy, who was aboard the <em>Planter</em> (Bodleian Library, <em>Rawlinson MS.</em> A 315). There is also in the Bodleian (<em>Rawlinson</em> A 299, f. 188) a second copy of the narrative here calendared, together with records of consultations, letters, accounts, etc.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-179" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-179" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Rawlinson</em> A 299.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-180" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-180" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A gold chain with a miniature of the King attached (Mundy). In a letter to the King of Spain in March, 1637 (<em>Lisbon Transcripts, Doc. Remett.</em>, bk. 37, f. 481) the Viceroy stated that he had delivered the present to the Vedor for transmission to Lisbon. It was not, however, dispatched till Oct., 1638 (<em>Ibid.</em>, bk. 41. f. 60).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-181" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-181" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1637, p. 74.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-182" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-182" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the account of this convent in Mr. Gray's edition of Pyrard de Laval (vol. ii. p. 58). It was rebuilt after the fire.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-183" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-183" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or Brockart (see above, p. 98).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-184" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-184" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The journal will be continued in the next volume.</span></li></ol></section>
